<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a deterministic algorithm that computes the discrete Fourier transform at every length <i>n</i> in $`O(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}})`$ operations. The model uses exact complex arithmetic, unrestricted coefficients, specified Fourier roots, and unit-cost logarithmic-size indexing; scalar preparation and array organization are included.

- **claim-uniform-exact-fourier** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a single deterministic algorithm which, for every positive integer $n$ and every $x\in\mathbb C^n$, computes $F_nx$ exactly. In the model above, including scalar preparation, schedule construction, and index work, its running time is $$\label{eq:main-bound} O\!\left(n(\log n)^\theta(\log\log n)^{4-\theta}\right)$$ as $n\to\infty$. It suffices to supply the specified root $\zeta_{D_*}$ for an explicitly computable integer $D_*<1024n^3$.
  - Explanation: A single deterministic procedure computes the exact Fourier transform at every positive length, charging scalar preparation, scheduling, indexing, and array organization in the specified arithmetic model.
- **claim-decimal-power-saving** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-decimal)): In the same model, the transform is computable at every length in $$O\!\left(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}}\right)=o(n\log n)$$ time.
  - Explanation: The exact exponent bound absorbs the additional iterated logarithmic factor and yields the stated conservative power saving for sufficiently large lengths.
- **claim-exact-bilinear-convolution** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-cor-polynomial-multiplication)): There is a single deterministic algorithm which, for every positive integer $n$ and every $a,b\in\mathbb C^n$, computes the linear convolution $$c_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le r,s<n\\r+s=k}}a_rb_s, \qquad 0\le k\le 2n-2.$$ Equivalently, it multiplies two complex polynomials of degree less than $n$. In the exact complex field-operation, specified-root, and address cost model above, with general field multiplication permitted for the pointwise products, the total running time, including scalar preparation, schedule construction, padding, and index work, is $$O\!\left(n(\log n)^\theta(\log\log n)^{4-\theta}\right) \qquad(n\to\infty),$$ and hence is eventually $O(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}})$. This is a bilinear arithmetic algorithm, not an input-linear circuit: its pointwise products multiply a value linear in $a$ by a value linear in $b$.
  - Explanation: Padding followed by Fourier transforms, pointwise products, reversal, and scaling computes linear convolution and complex polynomial multiplication. The variable pointwise products make this a bilinear algorithm.
- **claim-arbitrary-auxiliary-scalar-network** (construction, [source](#stmt-net-scalar)): For arbitrary initial auxiliary values, the forward invocation sends $(x,y,A,c)$ to $(x,y+x,A,c)$. Forward invocations from $X$ to $Y$ at stages $1$ and $3$, and the inverse forward invocation from $Y$ to $X$ at stage $2$, give $$(X_d,Y_d)\longmapsto(-Y_d,X_d) \quad(d\in\mathcal T^3)$$ and restore every auxiliary value.
  - Explanation: The invocation cancels the contribution of arbitrary auxiliary contents. Composing the prescribed bank updates gives the signed exchange and restores every auxiliary value.
- **claim-directional-frame-transition** (construction, [source](#stmt-net-edge)): Let $U,V\subseteq\mathcal D$ be nested nondegenerate subspaces. Call the orthogonal complement of the smaller subspace inside the larger their *residual*. Suppose it has an orthonormal binary basis of size $\rho$. Then $\Phi_V\Phi_U^{-1}$ is a product of exactly $\rho$ *directional kernels* of the form $C_z=aI+bR_z$ or $C_z^{-1}$, where $z\ne0$. Each inverse requires one forward kernel and a translation.
  - Explanation: An orthonormal basis of the residual decomposes a frame transition into directional kernels. Inverse kernels require a forward kernel and a translation.
- **claim-network-residual-budget** (bound, [source](#stmt-net-residuals)): Every edge has nested nondegenerate endpoint labels. Each nonzero orthogonal residual, meaning the complement of the smaller label inside the larger, has an orthonormal binary basis. If $s$ is the sum of the residual dimensions, then $$\begin{aligned} s&=Wm-2N_0+2I_0(101)100 =1873807244636671267308000000,\label{net:s}\\ \Delta:=Wm-s&=6871402692000000>0.\label{net:delta} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: The chosen labels make every edge transition admissible and give a strictly positive saving in the sum of residual dimensions relative to independent coordinate directions.
- **claim-all-role-finite-network** (construction, [source](#stmt-net-finite-interface)): For every integer $f\ge1$, the explicit network applies $C^{\otimes mf}$ to each of $W$ arbitrary arrays indexed by $\mathbb F_2^{mf}$. It uses $s$ directional steps, each on one physical array and conjugate by address permutations to copies of $C^{\otimes f}$ on fibers. All other work consists of a fixed number of pointwise linear maps and address permutations. The same assertion holds with any spectator address bits.
  - Explanation: The network transforms every physical array, including arrays used as auxiliary roles. Each directional step becomes a smaller tensor transform after address permutations.
- **claim-linear-time-binary-indexing** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-net-indexing)): Let $k=mf+r$ with $0\le r<m$, and fix an invertible binary map on an $m$-bit column. Applying it in all $f$ columns, translating columns by prescribed fixed vectors, and collecting one chosen output bit from every column into a contiguous $f$-bit address field can be implemented in $O(2^k)$ time. This includes all index preparation and data movement; the constant may depend on $m$ and the fixed maps, but not on $f$ or $k$.
  - Explanation: A digit prefix traversal applies the fixed column maps and gathers selected bits without scanning all columns at every array entry.
- **claim-fast-kernel-tensor-powers** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-net-tensor-bound)): There is a deterministic exact algorithm applying $C^{\otimes k}$ to an arbitrary complex array of length $2^k$ in $$O\bigl(2^k(k+1)^\theta\bigr)$$ time, for every integer $k\ge0$. The bound includes fixed-table construction, all scalar arithmetic, array initialization, index preparation, and data movement. Only the rational constants and $i$ are required.
  - Explanation: Padding the physical roles permits exact recursive batches. The resulting recurrence gives the tensor bound while charging initialization, arithmetic, indexing, and movement.
- **claim-uniform-exact-width-compiler** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-loc-compiler)): Let $r\ge1$ and let $u$ be any primitive $r$th root of unity. The matrix $F_r(u)=(u^{ij})_{0\le i,j<r}$ has a word on exactly $r$ coordinates with $O(1+\log^4r)$ slots, each of which is either* 1. *a monomial layer, or* 2. *disjoint copies of $C$ on ordered pairs, with identity on every remaining singleton.* *There is one fixed repeating pattern of slot types into which all these words can be padded. For any finite collection of widths at most $R$, the words can therefore be synchronized in $O(1+\log^4R)$ slots.* *Their schedules and scalar coefficients can be prepared in time $r^{O(1)}$, with an absolute exponent, counting both exact scalar arithmetic and integer bookkeeping. Besides $u$ and the fixed constant $i$, this preparation needs only power-of-two roots of orders at most $8r$. Coefficients are specified by shared arithmetic programs over these roots and rational numbers. Every division is by a proved nonzero scalar; every structural choice uses integers alone. Neither a test for zero of a complex number nor conjugation of input data is required.
  - Explanation: Every primitive-root Fourier matrix has an exact-width word in a common repeating pattern of monomial and kernel-pair slots. Its schedules and scalar programs are prepared effectively without testing complex coefficients for zero.
- **claim-newton-toeplitz-factorization** (construction, [source](#stmt-loc-newton-reduction)): For every primitive $r$th root $u$, with $r\ge1$, there is an invertible lower triangular matrix $N$ such that $$F_r(u)=N D_N^{-1}N^{\mathsf T}, \qquad D_N=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(N_{00},\ldots,N_{r-1,r-1}).$$ The matrix $N$ is a product of two invertible diagonal matrices and one invertible lower triangular Toeplitz matrix of width $r$. All these factors can be prepared in polynomial time as rational expressions in $u$, with every denominator nonzero.
  - Explanation: Evaluation in the Newton basis gives a triangular factor. Symmetry identifies the transpose factor, and diagonal scaling isolates an invertible triangular Toeplitz matrix.
- **claim-catalytic-linear-dag-replay** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-loc-replay)): Suppose $M:\mathbb C^e\to\mathbb C^a$ has a linear DAG with $g$ gates, depth $H$, fan-in at most two, and fan-out at most $\delta$, including output uses. Each output is a source or gate read, or zero. On disjoint coordinate sets $x,y,z$ of sizes $e,a,g$, respectively, a word of $O((\delta+1)(H+1))$ shear layers implements $$(x,y,z)\longmapsto(x,y+Mx,z).$$ It uses at most $8g+2a$ shears. The initial contents of $z$ are arbitrary, including values correlated with $x$, $y$, or other data.
  - Explanation: A forward replay and its reversal restore borrowed coordinates but leave an unwanted contribution in the target. A source-suppressed replay cancels that contribution, including when borrowed contents are correlated with other data.
- **claim-effective-exact-width-toeplitz** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-loc-toeplitz)): An invertible lower triangular Toeplitz matrix of width $r$ has a word on exactly $r$ coordinates in $O(1+\log^4r)$ mixed rounds. A fixed algorithm prepares the word and its coefficients in $r^{O(1)}$ time from the Toeplitz entries and power-of-two roots of orders at most $8r$. The topology depends only on $r$; all divisions by Toeplitz parameters are justified by the nonzero diagonal entry.
  - Explanation: Recursive diagonal blocks and convolution-based cross updates compile an invertible triangular Toeplitz matrix without increasing coordinate width. Counting the actual gate coordinates selects admissible chunks.
- **claim-fourier-monomial-shear-word** (construction, [source](#stmt-loc-fourier)): For every primitive $r$th root $u$, the matrix $F_r(u)$ has a polynomially preparable exact-width word in $O(1+\log^4r)$ monomial and shear layers. Its coefficients are rational expressions in $u$ and the power-of-two roots from Lemma <a href="#loc:toeplitz" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:toeplitz">14</a>, with all denominators nonzero.
  - Explanation: Composing the Toeplitz and diagonal factors with their transposes gives a polynomially preparable Fourier word on its original coordinates.
- **claim-linear-sector-packing** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-sector-address)): The start of the selected sector in the packed array is $$\label{eq:sector-start} S=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\left(\prod_{i<j}q_i\right)p_j\tau_j.$$ The packing permutation, its inverse, and the list of sector starts, widths, and pair counts can all be generated in $O(R)$ operations after the local tables and suffix products have been prepared.
  - Explanation: The sector-start formula counts earlier sectors by their first differing block choice. Maintaining prefix states generates packing, unpacking, and sector descriptors in linear array work.
- **claim-synchronized-tensor-fourier** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-prop-tensor-fourier)): Let $\ell\ge1$ and $r_1,\ldots,r_\ell\ge2$ be integers, and suppose the required specified roots are given. Put $R=\prod_jr_j$ and $r_{\max}=\max_jr_j$. The map $\bigotimes_jF_{r_j}$ is computable in $$\label{eq:tensor-fourier} O\!\left(R(\ell+1)^\theta(1+\log r_{\max})^4\right) +\mathop{\mathrm{poly}}(\ell,r_{\max},\log(R+2))$$ operations, including compilation, scalar preparation, and array work. The polynomial and the implicit constant are absolute.
  - Explanation: Synchronized local words reduce simultaneous pair slots to independent kernel tensor powers. Linear-time sector packing prevents an additional scan over the axes at every entry.
- **claim-small-prime-working-axes** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-prime-lengths)): One has $r_j=O((j+1)^2)$ and $$\log R_\ell=\Theta(\ell\log(\ell+2)).$$ For the largest $\ell$ with $R_\ell\le2n$, as $n\to\infty$, $$\label{eq:axis-count} \ell=\Theta\!\left(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\right), \qquad \log\ell=\Theta(\log\log n).$$ The primes and this value of $\ell$ can be found with a number of operations polynomial in $\log(n+2)$, using $O(\log(n+2))$-bit integers.
  - Explanation: Elementary prime estimates control both the widths and number of odd-prime axes. Trial division and product maintenance find the required axes with polynomial logarithmic work.
- **claim-local-finite-win-certificate** (construction, [source](#stmt-syn-local-certificate)): For the invertible nonmonomial matrix $C$ in <a href="#net:C" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="net:C">[net:C]</a>, there are integers $b\ge2$ and $g\ge0$ and a word on exactly $2^b$ coordinates which implements $C^{\otimes b}$ using $g<b2^{b-1}$ forward calls to $C$ and invertible monomials. On two coordinates, $I_2+E_{12}$ has a word with exactly three forward calls to the same $C$ and invertible diagonal factors.
  - Explanation: The finite network supplies a tensor word with fewer forward kernel calls than separate axis evaluation. The explicit shear identity supplies the companion elementary shear word.
- **claim-effective-rational-algebra-extraction** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-syn-extraction)): Given finitely many polynomials $f_i$ in $a$ variables over $\mathbb Q$, a deterministic rational algorithm either certifies that $(f_i)$ is the unit ideal or produces a nonzero finite-dimensional commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra $E$, with a rational multiplication table, and elements $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_a\in E$ satisfying every $f_i(\xi)=0$. In particular, a complex solution guarantees the second outcome. Required units can be imposed by adding inverse-witness equations.
  - Explanation: A terminating rational procedure either certifies inconsistency by the unit ideal or constructs a nonzero finite algebra satisfying all polynomial equations. Inverse witnesses enforce required units without selecting a complex embedding.
- **claim-bounded-printer-uniformization** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-syn-printer)): Suppose a fixed bit machine prints a sequence of complete records $(m_t,C_t)$ with strictly increasing $m_t\to\infty$, where $C_t$ computes $F_{m_t}$ by $s_t=o(m_t\log m_t)$ scalar linear gates. Each record must explicitly list the gates, output indices, and a scalar preparation program with every instruction explicitly listed and executed once in order. Its instructions use rational literals, field operations with valid nonzero divisors, and canonical Fourier roots $\zeta_a=\exp(2\pi i/a)$ of positive integer orders whose orders are written literally in binary. Then there is one deterministic algorithm computing every $F_n$ in $o(n\log n)$ time, including preparation and indexing in the model of this paper. A single specified root of order less than $8n^3$ suffices.
  - Explanation: A bounded simulation selects a completed small radix record. Reusing its explicit circuit in mixed-radix transforms and a chirp reduction yields a uniform all-length algorithm despite unrestricted printing time.

      "exact_statement": "Let $r\\ge1$ and let $u$ be any primitive $r$th root of unity.         "exact_quote": "Suppose a fixed bit machine prints a sequence of complete records $(m_t,C_t)$ with strictly increasing $m_t\\to\\infty$, where $C_t$ computes $F_{m_t}$ by $s_t=o(m_t\\log m_t)$ scalar linear gates. Each record must explicitly list the gates, output indices, and a scalar preparation program with every instruction explicitly listed and executed once in order. Its instructions use rational literals, field operations with valid nonzero divisors, and canonical Fourier roots $\\zeta_a=\\exp(2\\pi i/a)$ of positive integer orders whose orders are written literally in binary. Then there is one deterministic algorithm computing every $F_n$ in $o(n\\log n)$ time, including preparation and indexing in the model of this paper. A single specified root of order less than $8n^3$ suffices.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Unnormalized discrete Fourier matrix** ([source](#pm-h-2)): For $d\ge1$, put $\zeta_d=\exp(2\pi i/d)$ and let $$F_d=(\zeta_d^{jk})_{0\le j,k<d}$$ be the unnormalized discrete Fourier matrix.
- **Fixed kernel C** ([source](#net:C)): Put $$\label{net:C} C=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&a\end{pmatrix}, \qquad a=\frac{1+i}{2},\qquad b=\frac{1-i}{2}.$$
- **Nondegenerate binary subspace** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A subspace is *nondegenerate* if its restricted bilinear form has zero radical.
- **Shear** ([source](#loc:section)): A *shear* adds a scalar multiple of one coordinate to a distinct coordinate and leaves all other coordinates unchanged.
- **Shear layer** ([source](#loc:section)): A *shear layer* consists of shears on disjoint pairs.
- **Monomial layer** ([source](#loc:section)): A *monomial layer* is an invertible monomial matrix.
- **Mixed round** ([source](#loc:section)): We also use *mixed rounds*: on disjoint coordinate regions, such a round performs monomial layers or shear layers.
- **Linear arithmetic DAG** ([source](#pm-h-14)): We use linear arithmetic directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) in which each gate forms a linear combination of at most two sources or earlier gate values.
- **Orthogonal residual** ([source](#stmt-net-edge)): Call the orthogonal complement of the smaller subspace inside the larger their *residual*.
- **Sector** ([source](#pm-h-19)): A *sector* chooses one block on each axis.
- **Odd-prime working product** ([source](#pm-h-22)): Let $r_1<r_2<\cdots$ be the odd primes and let $R_\ell=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}r_j$, with $R_0=1$.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-fixed-binary-block | Binary block dimension m | = | $10^6$ | Absolute constant independent of the requested transform length. |
| q-padded-role-count | Padded physical role count W_* | = | $2^{71}$ | Absolute constant. |
| q-dimension-saving | Residual dimension saving Delta | = | $6871402692000000$ | Absolute positive saving. |
| q-recurrence-multiplier | Normalized recurrence multiplier lambda | = | $m-\frac{\Delta}{W_*}$ | Absolute constant determined by the finite network. |
| q-critical-exponent | Tensor recurrence exponent theta | = | $\log_m\lambda$ | Absolute constant; the displayed decimal is only indicative. |
| q-physical-wire-count | Physical wire count W | = | $1873807244643542670000$ | Absolute finite network size. |
| q-residual-dimension-sum | Sum of edge residual dimensions s | = | $1873807244636671267308000000$ | Absolute directional-step count. |
| q-all-length-fourier-time | Charged exact Fourier running time | = | $O\!\left(n(\log n)^\theta(\log\log n)^{4-\theta}\right)$ | As the positive transform length tends to infinity; preparation and organization are included. |
| q-main-root-order | Order D_* of the specified Fourier root | < | $1024n^3$ | Positive input length n. |
| q-decimal-fourier-time | Conservative asymptotic Fourier running time | = | $O\!\left(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}}\right)$ | Eventually as the transform length tends to infinity. |
| q-kernel-tensor-time | Time to apply the kernel tensor power | = | $O\bigl(2^k(k+1)^\theta\bigr)$ | All nonnegative tensor orders; includes initialization and data movement. |
| q-binary-address-permutation-time | Time for the fixed binary column address changes | = | $O(2^k)$ | The implicit constant may depend on the fixed block dimension and maps, but not on the column count or total address width. |
| q-local-compiler-slots | Number of synchronized local compiler slots | = | $O(1+\log^4r)$ | Local width r. |
| q-replay-shear-count | Number of shears in arbitrary borrowed-coordinate replay | ≤ | $8g+2a$ | The gate count and target dimension of the supplied linear DAG. |
| q-replay-layer-count | Number of shear layers for replay | = | $O((\delta+1)(H+1))$ | DAG depth and fan-out, including designated output uses. |
| q-toeplitz-mixed-rounds | Exact-width Toeplitz mixed-round depth | = | $O(1+\log^4r)$ | Local Toeplitz width. |
| q-sector-packing-time | Sector packing, inverse packing, and descriptor generation | = | $O(R)$ | After preparation of local tables and suffix products. |
| q-working-length-upper-bound | Working transform length L | < | $4n$ | Positive requested Fourier length. |
| q-working-length-lower-bound | Working transform length L | ≥ | $2n$ | Positive requested Fourier length. |
| q-printer-root-order | Specified root order in bounded-printer uniformization | < | $8n^3$ | The all-length algorithm supplied by the bounded-printer theorem. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-scalar-cancellation** [claim](#stmt-net-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-net-scalar): Cancel the auxiliary contributions in the invocation and compose the signed bank updates. -> An exact bank exchange restoring all auxiliary values.
- **step-directional-frames** [claim](#stmt-net-edge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-net-edge): Use orthogonal weight additivity and Walsh conjugation to factor a frame transition. -> Directional kernels for the frame ratio, with inverse corrections by translation.
- **step-residual-saving** [claim](#stmt-net-residuals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-net-residuals): Check the residual list, telescope signed dimension changes, charge internal decreases, and construct orthonormal bases. -> Admissible edge transitions and a strictly positive residual dimension saving.
- **step-finite-all-role-interface** [claim](#stmt-net-finite-interface) / [proof](#proof-stmt-net-finite-interface): Telescope common gate frames, correct terminal translations and bank exchange, and conjugate directional steps to fibers. -> The desired tensor transform on every arbitrary physical role.
- **step-binary-prefix-indexing** [claim](#stmt-net-indexing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-net-indexing): Traverse column digits while retaining partial source and destination addresses. -> Linear array work for address permutations and contiguous fiber grouping.
- **step-exact-tensor-batching** [claim](#stmt-net-tensor-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-net-tensor-bound): Pad role counts to a power of two, recurse on complete batches, and solve the normalized recurrence at its critical exponent. -> The charged kernel tensor-power algorithm.
- **step-newton-reduction** [claim](#stmt-loc-newton-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-newton-reduction): Evaluate the Newton polynomial basis and use uniqueness of triangular factorization with Fourier symmetry. -> Diagonal and triangular Toeplitz factors for the Fourier matrix.
- **step-borrowed-coordinate-replay** [claim](#stmt-loc-replay) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-replay): Replay and reverse the DAG, then repeat without direct-source edges to cancel the borrowed-value contribution. -> The target linear update with all arbitrary borrowed values restored.
- **step-exact-width-toeplitz** [claim](#stmt-loc-toeplitz) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-toeplitz): Split diagonal blocks, reconstruct cross chunks from displacement outer products, and select chunk sizes by actual gate counts. -> A polynomially prepared exact-width word with the stated mixed-round depth.
- **step-fourier-shear-layers** [claim](#stmt-loc-fourier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-fourier): Compose the Toeplitz word and diagonal factors with their transposes. -> A polynomially prepared Fourier word of monomial and shear layers.
- **step-common-kernel-slots** [claim](#stmt-loc-compiler) / [proof](#loc:three-kernel): Replace shears by fixed kernel words, split potentially vanishing coefficients into nonzero summands, and pad into a common slot pattern. -> Synchronized monomial and kernel-pair slots with all scalar preparation charged.
- **step-sector-packing** [claim](#stmt-lem-sector-address) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sector-address): Classify preceding sectors by the first differing axis and update prefix states during traversal. -> Packed contiguous sectors and their descriptors in linear array work.
- **step-synchronized-tensor-transform** [claim](#stmt-prop-tensor-fourier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-tensor-fourier): Synchronize local words, traverse tensor monomials, and apply the kernel algorithm to each packed sector. -> The charged tensor-product Fourier transform bound.
- **step-small-prime-axes** [claim](#stmt-lem-prime-lengths) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prime-lengths): Bound primes using the central binomial coefficient and find the maximal admissible initial prime product by elementary integer arithmetic. -> Small coprime axes, their count estimates, and polynomial logarithmic selection work.
- **step-uniform-all-length-transform** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prime-lengths-2): Choose the working length, apply coprime tensor indexing and chirp convolution, supply all roots from the master root, and substitute the axis-count estimates. -> The deterministic exact Fourier algorithm at every positive length with the main running-time bound.
- **step-conservative-decimal-bound** [claim](#stmt-cor-decimal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prime-lengths-2): Bound the relative recurrence saving by exact integer inequalities and absorb the fixed iterated logarithmic factor. -> The conservative decimal logarithmic power saving.
- **step-bilinear-convolution** [claim](#stmt-cor-polynomial-multiplication) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prime-lengths-3): Pad both operands, transform, multiply pointwise, and recover the linear convolution by reversal and scaling. -> Exact linear convolution and polynomial multiplication with the stated charged bound.
- **step-local-finite-certificate** [claim](#stmt-syn-local-certificate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-syn-local-certificate): Convert directional and scalar work to forward kernel calls, then enlarge the tensor order until the strict saving dominates fixed overhead. -> A finite tensor-saving word and an elementary shear word for the same kernel.
- **step-finite-rational-extraction** [claim](#stmt-syn-extraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-syn-extraction): Compute finite fibers by rational linear algebra and introduce triangular monic relations whenever a zero fiber requires another elimination step. -> A unit-ideal certificate or a nonzero finite rational algebra satisfying the equations.
- **step-bounded-printer-uniformization** [claim](#stmt-syn-printer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-syn-printer): Bound printer simulation, select a completed small radix, decode explicit scalar programs, and use mixed-radix transforms inside chirp convolution. -> A uniform all-length exact Fourier algorithm with preparation and indexing included.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **sector-start**: The weighted prefix formula gives the starting address of each selected sector in the packed array.
- **packing-permutation**: Packing and unpacking preserve every array entry through mutually inverse address permutations.
- **sector-tensor-shape**: Each sector has power-of-two width determined by its chosen pairs and carries the corresponding tensor power of the fixed kernel.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **avoid-axis-scan**: Recompute the sector address by scanning all axes separately for every array entry while claiming the source's linear traversal bound. -> The implementation introduces repeated axis work that the prefix traversal explicitly avoids.
- **avoid-consecutive-pair-assumption**: Treat a pair's local position as its original axis digit. -> Nonconsecutive or reversed pair coordinates are sent to incorrect packed addresses.
- **avoid-global-certification**: Use finite address-packing tests as certification of the complete Fourier algorithm and its asymptotic operation bound. -> Address agreement does not execute the finite network, local Fourier compiler, scalar preparation, or recursive tensor transforms.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The sector-address lemma supplies a finite constructive packing permutation. The executable checks its address formula, inverse permutation, and sector metadata on finite local block tables. It does not certify the full Fourier construction or its asymptotic bound.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON object with axes, each an ordered list of singleton or ordered-pair blocks.
# PRECONDITIONS: At least one axis; each axis partitions its consecutive integer digits and has radix at least two.
# OUTPUTS: JSON mapping from original addresses to packed addresses, inverse mapping, and sector start/width/pair-count triples.
# TIME: O(R) address work under the source's integer-word model, after local tables and suffix products are prepared.
# SPACE: Linear address tables and output arrays, with a depth-first traversal stack.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact integer arithmetic for addresses, widths, and counts.
# FAILURE: Invalid block partitions raise an exception; no partial result is emitted.
import json
import sys


def pack(data):
    axes = data['axes']
    if not isinstance(axes, list) or not axes:
        raise ValueError('missing axes')
    tables = []
    block_tables = []
    radices = []
    for blocks in axes:
        if not isinstance(blocks, list) or not blocks:
            raise ValueError('missing blocks')
        flat = []
        for block in blocks:
            if not isinstance(block, list) or len(block) not in (1, 2):
                raise ValueError('block width')
            if any(type(digit) is not int for digit in block):
                raise ValueError('digit type')
            flat.extend(block)
        r = len(flat)
        if r < 2 or sorted(flat) != list(range(r)):
            raise ValueError('partition')
        table = [None] * r
        descriptors = []
        p = 0
        for block in blocks:
            q = len(block)
            descriptors.append((p, q))
            for t, digit in enumerate(block):
                table[digit] = (p, q, t)
            p += q
        tables.append(table)
        block_tables.append(descriptors)
        radices.append(r)
    ell = len(axes)
    suffix = [1] * ell
    total = 1
    for j in range(ell - 1, -1, -1):
        suffix[j] = total
        total *= radices[j]
    mapping = [None] * total
    inverse = [None] * total

    def digits(j, S, Q, o, I):
        if j == ell:
            address = S + o
            mapping[I] = address
            if not 0 <= address < total or inverse[address] is not None:
                raise ValueError('packing collision')
            inverse[address] = I
            return
        for digit, (p, q, t) in enumerate(tables[j]):
            digits(j + 1, S + Q * p * suffix[j], Q * q,
                   o * q + t, I * radices[j] + digit)

    digits(0, 0, 1, 0, 0)
    sectors = []

    def blocks(j, S, Q, pairs):
        if j == ell:
            sectors.append([S, Q, pairs])
            return
        for p, q in block_tables[j]:
            blocks(j + 1, S + Q * p * suffix[j], Q * q,
                   pairs + (q == 2))

    blocks(0, 0, 1, 0)
    return {'mapping': mapping, 'inverse': inverse, 'sectors': sectors}


if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(json.dumps(pack(json.load(sys.stdin)), sort_keys=True))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-exact-field-cost** (formal_scope): The running-time guarantee charges exact complex field operations at unit cost. It does not establish the cost of representing or manipulating those values in bits. Blocked use: ['Report the arithmetic operation bound as an integer bit-complexity bound.']
- **limit-specified-root-input** (hypothesis): The algorithm requires a specified Fourier root. Generating roots of unbounded order from rational constants by field operations is not assumed. Blocked use: ['Implement the construction using rational constants alone while retaining the stated preparation bound.']
- **limit-unrestricted-coefficients-and-stability** (formal_scope): Complex coefficients and intermediate magnitudes are unrestricted, and no finite-precision or numerical-stability guarantee is supplied. Blocked use: ['Claim a stable floating-point Fourier improvement.', 'Transfer the operation bound to a bounded-coefficient implementation without further analysis.']
- **limit-asymptotic-crossover** (unknown): The construction has enormous fixed constants and supplies no useful crossover estimate. Blocked use: ['Claim a practical speedup at a specified finite transform length.', 'Infer a real-time processing guarantee from the asymptotic saving.']
- **limit-integer-address-model** (formal_scope): Integer operations and random access have the stated unit cost only within the logarithmic-size word convention. Blocked use: ['Transfer the array-work bound to an execution model with different address or memory-access costs without accounting for them.']
- **limit-arbitrary-role-restoration** (hypothesis): Recursive batching needs a full linear-map identity on arbitrary contents in every physical role. Restoration proved only for initially zero auxiliary data is insufficient. Blocked use: ['Replace the scalar network by a zero-workspace-only identity and reuse arbitrary transform fibers as its auxiliary roles.']
- **limit-orthonormal-residuals** (hypothesis): The directional frame decomposition requires nested nondegenerate labels and an orthonormal binary basis of their residual. Nondegeneracy alone does not establish that basis property. Blocked use: ['Apply the directional kernel count to an arbitrary nondegenerate binary symmetric residual without checking whether it admits the required basis.']
- **limit-complete-recursive-batches** (hypothesis): The recurrence uses the padded role count and exact divisibility of the smaller-transform fibers into complete batches. Blocked use: ['Allow partially filled recursive batches while retaining the stated recurrence multiplier.']
- **limit-fixed-column-maps** (hypothesis): The binary address-change bound treats the column dimension and maps as fixed. Its implicit constant may depend on those fixed choices. Blocked use: ['Vary the column dimension with the input and assume the same uniform address-permutation constant.']
- **limit-primitive-local-roots** (hypothesis): The Newton reduction and local Fourier compiler require a primitive root of the requested order. Their nonzero-denominator argument uses primitivity. Blocked use: ['Substitute a root of smaller order into the Newton factors and perform the same divisions.']
- **limit-replay-interface** (hypothesis): The replay construction assumes a linear DAG with the stated fan-in, fan-out, and output-read convention, together with disjoint source, target, and borrowed coordinate sets. Blocked use: ['Apply the shear count to nonlinear gates.', 'Ignore designated output uses when counting fan-out.', 'Overlap source or target coordinates with the borrowed gate coordinates.']
- **limit-toeplitz-invertibility-and-fit** (hypothesis): The Toeplitz compiler assumes a nonzero diagonal and accepts a chunk size only after counting the coordinates required by its actual convolution graphs. If none fits, the source provides a direct word. Blocked use: ['Divide by a zero Toeplitz diagonal.', 'Borrow coordinates beyond the parent block.', 'Assume an asymptotic gate estimate alone proves a selected chunk fits.']
- **limit-exact-local-width** (hypothesis): Local Fourier words preserve their original coordinate widths. Padding every tensor factor independently can enlarge the total product dimension. Blocked use: ['Add fresh auxiliary coordinates to each local factor while retaining the original tensor-size bound.']
- **limit-prepared-conjugates** (formal_scope): Conjugates used to split shear coefficients are prepared from scalar arithmetic programs by replacing roots with their inverses. This does not authorize conjugation of variable input data. Blocked use: ['Insert input-data conjugation into the claimed input-linear Fourier computation.', 'Assume the coefficient construction requires a complex zero test.']
- **limit-sector-interface-and-traversal** (hypothesis): Sector packing uses ordered pairs and singletons with local tables and suffix products already prepared. Its linear array-work argument carries state through prefix traversal. Blocked use: ['Assume pair coordinates are consecutive in their original order.', 'Rescan every axis at each entry and claim the same traversal bound.', 'Treat finite address tests as verification of the complete Fourier algorithm.']
- **limit-working-length-and-coprimality** (hypothesis): The working transform uses pairwise coprime factors and a sufficiently long padded convolution array. These conditions justify the tensor indexing and prevent chirp residues from colliding. Blocked use: ['Use overlapping factors in the coprime tensor factorization.', 'Shorten the chirp array without proving that its signed-offset residues remain distinct.']
- **limit-charged-preparation** (formal_scope): The algorithm charges local compilation, scalar preparation, fixed-kernel transformation, schedule construction, initialization, and array movement. Precomputation is not an uncharged resource. Blocked use: ['Omit the transform of the fixed chirp operand from the operation count.', 'Report only data gates while treating scalar programs and address organization as free.']
- **limit-bilinear-convolution-scope** (formal_scope): The polynomial multiplication consequence permits general field multiplication for variable pointwise products. It is bilinear and retains the exact complex arithmetic assumptions. Blocked use: ['Classify the two-input multiplication algorithm as an input-linear circuit.', 'Infer a finite-field polynomial multiplication result or an integer bit-complexity improvement.']
- **limit-finite-algebra-output** (formal_scope): The extraction procedure returns a finite commutative unital rational algebra, which need not be a field. Required units must be imposed through inverse-witness equations. Blocked use: ['Assume every nonzero returned algebra element is invertible.', 'Treat the multiplication table as a selected complex embedding.']
- **limit-synthesis-time** (unknown): The effective synthesis route proves termination but does not provide a useful compilation-time bound. Its printer may perform any finite amount of bit computation before producing a complete record. Blocked use: ['Assign a polynomial-time bound to the symbolic certificate search.', "Use the appendix's existence route as evidence for the main construction's explicit exponent without its separate analysis."]
- **limit-complete-printer-records** (hypothesis): Bounded-printer uniformization requires complete explicit circuit records, an increasing unbounded sequence of widths, the stated asymptotic gate saving, valid scalar divisions, and literal canonical root orders. Blocked use: ['Replace explicit gate lists with succinct descriptions that require uncharged expansion.', 'Hide loops or unspecified complex constants inside a scalar preparation instruction.', 'Apply the theorem to a finite sequence of efficient circuits.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 130 (`lean/docs/130.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Finite tensor savings and exact Fourier circuits](../../preprints/Finite-tensor-savings-and-exact-Fourier-circuits-September-25-2026/main.pdf)
> - [An explicit power saving for the exact discrete Fourier transform](../../preprints/An-explicit-power-saving-for-the-exact-discrete-Fourier-transform-September-25-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized result gives exact discrete Fourier transforms with arbitrarily small normalized circuit cost along an unbounded sequence of lengths. For every $c>0$ and every cutoff $N_0\ge2$, some $n\ge N_0$ has a circuit computing the unnormalized DFT with fewer than $cn\log_2 n$ gates. Addition, subtraction, and multiplication by a predetermined complex scalar each cost one gate; diagonal scalings are charged. The result is subsequential, with no all-length, bounded-coefficient, conditioning, or bit-complexity claim.
>
> A separate uniform formalization gives fixed deterministic programs for every positive length $n$, computing the canonical discrete Fourier transform and the full convolution of two length-$n$ complex vectors exactly. Their work is $O(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}})=o(n\log n)$, including root-order selection and scalar preparation. The chosen root of unity is supplied; the model permits exact complex arithmetic and unrestricted coefficients, while integer values and indices remain polynomially bounded in $n$. The synthesis appendix's extraction, search, and printer uniformization is outside these selected statements.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace ExactFourier
noncomputable def zeta (n : ℕ) : ℂ := Complex.exp (2 * Real.pi * Complex.I / (n : ℂ))
end ExactFourier

/-!
# Exact scalar and index RAM used for the algorithm

Finite, first-order, expression/program syntax. Arrays below are random-access arrays of
registers (an array-valued register holds its address). An expression building an array uses
a fresh array; `sow` builds by a loop of writes instead of a loop supplying consecutive
entries. Programs have their usual sequential stack evaluation. Evaluation here records work
(including stack, index and array work), the high water mark on produced/logically looped-over
integer values (addresses *within* arrays are integers too), the value, and the side condition
required along the executed path (in particular no division by zero). The charged units of
work are constant time; as usual constant rescalings between ways to charge a fixed number
of scalar or address operations are immaterial.

Enough address bits must be provided BOTH for the integer high water mark (and inputs), AND
for the work bound together with input size, as will be required in the theorem. Indeed we
use the particularly simple RAM convention where temporaries aren't reused: one work unit can
leave at most a constant number of cells (stack, pairs or array cells); whenever many array
entries are made their number is charged. So a constant number of words of those sizes also
suffices for arena addresses. Nothing charges constant work for changing an already published
array to a different array as a whole. Register and stack layouts, and the convention whether
registers hold pairs or addresses of pairs, only have constant factors (the program is fixed).

Scalar and data field registers are separated. Tests and loop/request sizes are on nonnegative
integer registers. There is no instruction for an arbitrary complex constant. A supplied
specified root will be an input. Divisions are on prepared scalars; valid runs require every
such denominator nonzero; the side condition is *not* a test performed by the algorithm.
The Bool mode on the syntax allows or prohibits general products (two-input convolution
versus the transform); even when allowed we use separate register kinds before and after
the bilinear products.

Definitions are mathematical operation-count semantics, not a model of running Lean code.
E.g. a function on Fin n represents the n cells of a random-access array, not a function
closure at runtime.
-/

namespace PowerSaving

universe u v

variable {α : Type u} {β : Type v}

/-- Register arrays. -/
structure Tape (α : Type u) where
  len : ℕ
  pos : Fin len → α

namespace Tape

def empty (α : Type u) : Tape α := ⟨0, Fin.elim0⟩

/-- Materialized array. -/
def tab (n : ℕ) (f : ℕ → α) : Tape α := ⟨n, fun i => f i.val⟩

/-- Read with fixed out of bounds default. -/
def look (v : Tape α) (i : ℕ) (z : α) : α :=
  if h : i < v.len then v.pos ⟨i,h⟩ else z

@[simp] theorem look_of_lt (v : Tape α) (z : α) {i} (h : i < v.len) :
    v.look i z = v.pos ⟨i,h⟩ := by simp [look, h]

@[simp] theorem look_of_le (v : Tape α) (z : α) {i} (h : v.len ≤ i) :
    v.look i z = z := by simp [look, Nat.not_lt.mpr h]

/-- Semantics of a write into fresh storage; out-of-range writes are ignored. Only used
  during construction, never a whole-tape functional update instruction. -/
def set (v : Tape α) (i : ℕ) (x : α) : Tape α :=
  ⟨v.len, fun j => if j.val = i then x else v.pos j⟩

def sow (len count : ℕ) (z : α) (f : ℕ → ℕ × α) : Tape α :=
  iter count
where
  iter : ℕ → Tape α
  | 0 => tab len (fun _ => z)
  | i+1 => (iter i).set (f i).1 (f i).2

def map (f : α → β) (v : Tape α) : Tape β := ⟨v.len, fun i => f (v.pos i)⟩

end Tape

namespace RAM

inductive Paint
  | scalar
  | left
  | right
  | both

inductive Ty
  | w
  | c : Paint → Ty
  | p : Ty → Ty → Ty
  | a : Ty → Ty

open Ty

abbrev sc := c .scalar

@[reducible] def Ty.T : Ty → Type
  | w => ℕ
  | c _ => ℂ
  | p s b => s.T × b.T
  | a b => Tape b.T

noncomputable def Ty.blank : (t : Ty) → t.T
  | w => 0
  | c _ => 0
  | p s t => (s.blank, t.blank)
  | a b => Tape.empty b.T

/-- One basic work unit below pays for O(1) primitive RAM operations; includes loop and
  stack overhead. `peak` tracks produced/needed integer quantities beyond inputs
  (total allocation for stack and array bases is covered separately by work). -/
structure Bill (α : Type u) where
  val : α
  work : ℕ
  peak : ℕ
  valid : Prop

namespace Bill

/-- Charge some work and optionally a new word bound. -/
def pay (b : Bill α) (n : ℕ) (m : ℕ) : Bill α :=
  ⟨b.val, b.work+n, max b.peak m, b.valid⟩

/-- Both computations are evaluated. -/
def pass (b : Bill α) (f : α → Bill β) : Bill β :=
  let d := f b.val
  ⟨d.val, b.work+d.work, max b.peak d.peak, b.valid ∧ d.valid⟩

def one (x : α) : Bill α := ⟨x,1,0,True⟩
def word (x : ℕ) : Bill ℕ := ⟨x,1,x,True⟩

/-- Execute f 0 through f (n-1), iterating an accumulator. -/
def steps (init : α) (f : ℕ → α → Bill α) : ℕ → Bill α
  | 0 => Bill.one init
  | i+1 => ((steps init f i).pass (f i)).pay 1 (i+1)

/-- Build Tape len by count writes (initialized with a blank z). -/
def sow (len count : ℕ) (z : α) (f : ℕ → Bill (ℕ × α)) : Bill (Tape α) :=
  (steps (Tape.tab len (fun _ => z))
    (fun i v => (f i).pass (fun s => one (v.set s.1 s.2))) count).pay (1+len) len

/-- Build Tape len. As for sow we can first allocate then perform writes. -/
def tab (len : ℕ) (z : α) (f : ℕ → Bill α) : Bill (Tape α) :=
  sow len len z (fun i => (f i).pass (fun y => one (i,y)))

end Bill

inductive NOp
  | add | sub | mul | div | mod | lt  -- truncated subtraction; compare returns 1 or 0

inductive Atom (allow : Bool) : Ty → Ty → Type
  | lit {a} (n : ℕ) : Atom allow a w
  | int (f : NOp) : Atom allow (p w w) w
  | cz (t) {a} : Atom allow a (c t)
  | cone {a} : Atom allow a sc
  | add (t) : Atom allow (p (c t) (c t)) (c t)
  | sub (t) : Atom allow (p (c t) (c t)) (c t)
  | scale (t) : Atom allow (p sc (c t)) (c t)
  | inv : Atom allow sc sc
  | cross (h : allow = true) :
      Atom allow (p (c .left) (c .right)) (c .both)
  | id {a} : Atom allow a a
  | fst {a b} : Atom allow (p a b) a
  | snd {a b} : Atom allow (p a b) b
  | len {s} : Atom allow (a s) w
  | look {s} : Atom allow (p (a s) w) s

noncomputable def NOp.run : NOp → (ℕ × ℕ) → Bill ℕ
  | .add, (i,j) => Bill.word (i+j)
  | .sub, (i,j) => Bill.word (i-j)
  | .mul, (i,j) => Bill.word (i*j)
  | .div, (i,j) => Bill.word (i/j) -- includes usual guard (returns zero)
  | .mod, (i,j) => Bill.word (i%j) -- like Lean: returns i if j=0
  | .lt, (i,j) => Bill.word (if i<j then 1 else 0)

noncomputable def Atom.run {allow a b} : Atom allow a b → a.T → Bill b.T
  | .lit n, _ => Bill.word n
  | .int f, x => f.run x
  | .cz _, _ => Bill.one 0
  | .cone, _ => Bill.one 1
  | .add _, (x,y) => Bill.one (x+y)
  | .sub _, (x,y) => Bill.one (x-y)
  | .scale _, (x,y) => Bill.one (x*y)
  | .inv, x => ⟨x⁻¹,1,0,x ≠ 0⟩
  | .cross _, (x,y) => Bill.one (x*y)
  | .id, x => Bill.one x
  | .fst, x => Bill.one x.1
  | .snd, x => Bill.one x.2
  | .len, x => Bill.word x.len
  | @Atom.look _ s, (x,i) => Bill.one (x.look i s.blank)

abbrev Port := Option (Ty × Ty)

/-- `call` is internal for bounded recursion; a program has Port = none. `loop`, `tab`
  and `sow` retain the input to access parameters in the body. `descend` sets up bounded
  recursion, reading fuel and initial argument. -/
inductive Code (allow : Bool) : Port → Ty → Ty → Type
  | atom {r s t} (op : Atom allow s t) : Code allow r s t
  | comp {r s t u} (f : Code allow r s t) (g : Code allow r t u) : Code allow r s u
  | fork {r s t u} (f : Code allow r s t) (g : Code allow r s u) : Code allow r s (p t u)
  | ifz {r s t} (q : Code allow r s w) (f g : Code allow r s t) : Code allow r s t
  | loop {r s t} (n : Code allow r s w) (init : Code allow r s t)
      (body : Code allow r (p s (p w t)) t) : Code allow r s t
  | tab {r s t} (n : Code allow r s w)
      (body : Code allow r (p s w) t) : Code allow r s (a t)
  | sow {r s t} (n count : Code allow r s w)
      (body : Code allow r (p s w) (p w t)) : Code allow r s (a t)
  | importClosed {r s t} (f : Code allow none s t) : Code allow r s t
  | call {s t} : Code allow (some (s,t)) s t
  | descend {r s t} (base : Code allow none s t)
      (body : Code allow (some (s,t)) s t) : Code allow r (p w s) t

@[reducible] def Handler : Port → Type
  | none => Unit
  | some (s,t) => s.T → Bill t.T

def depthRun (base : α → Bill β)
    (step : (α → Bill β) → (α → Bill β)) : ℕ → α → Bill β
  | 0, x => (base x).pay 1 0
  | n+1, x => (step (depthRun base step n) x).pay 1 (n+1)

noncomputable def Code.run {allow r s t} : Code allow r s t → Handler r → s.T → Bill t.T
  | .atom op, _, x => op.run x
  | .comp f g, h, x => ((f.run h x).pass (g.run h)).pay 1 0
  | .fork f g, h, x => (f.run h x).pass (fun y => (g.run h x).pass (fun z => Bill.one (y,z)))
  | .ifz q f g, h, x => ((q.run h x).pass (fun y =>
      if (y : ℕ) = 0 then f.run h x else g.run h x)).pay 1 0
  | .loop n init body, h, x =>
      ((n.run h x).pass (fun l => (init.run h x).pass (fun y =>
        Bill.steps y (fun i z => body.run h (x,(i,z))) l))).pay 1 0
  | @Code.tab _ _ _ t n body, h, x =>
      ((n.run h x).pass (fun l =>
        Bill.tab l t.blank (fun i => body.run h (x,i)))).pay 1 0
  | @Code.sow _ _ _ t n count body, h, x =>
      ((n.run h x).pass (fun l => (count.run h x).pass (fun k =>
        Bill.sow l k t.blank (fun i => body.run h (x,i))))).pay 1 0
  | .importClosed f, _, x => (f.run () x).pay 1 0
  | .call, h, x => (h x).pay 1 0
  | .descend base step, _, (k,x) =>
      (depthRun (base.run ()) (step.run) k x).pay 1 k

abbrev Prog (allow : Bool) (s t : Ty) := Code allow none s t

noncomputable abbrev run {allow s t} (f : Prog allow s t) (x : s.T) : Bill t.T :=
  f.run () x

end RAM
end PowerSaving


namespace PowerSaving
noncomputable section
open RAM RAM.Ty Filter Asymptotics

def paperM : ℝ := 1000000
def paperW : ℝ := ((2 : ℕ) : ℝ) ^ (71:ℕ)
def paperDelta : ℝ := 6871402692000000
def paperMultiplier := paperM - paperDelta / paperW
def theta : ℝ := Real.log paperMultiplier / Real.log paperM
def decimalExponent : ℝ := 1 - 1/(10^(13:ℕ))

def paperTime (n : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  (n:ℝ) * (Real.log (n:ℝ)) ^ theta * (Real.log (Real.log (n:ℝ))) ^ (4-theta)

def decimalTime (n : ℕ) : ℝ := (n:ℝ) * (Real.log (n:ℝ)) ^ decimalExponent

def nlogn (n : ℕ) : ℝ := (n:ℝ) * Real.log (n:ℝ)

abbrev root := ExactFourier.zeta

abbrev Vec (n : ℕ) := Fin n → ℂ

def dft (n : ℕ) (x : Vec n) : Vec n :=
  fun j => ∑ k, (root n) ^ (j.val*k.val) * x k

def conv (n : ℕ) (x y : Vec n) : Vec (2*n-1) :=
  fun k => ∑ r : Fin n, ∑ s : Fin n, if r.val+s.val=k.val then x r*y s else 0

abbrev dftInKind := p w (p sc (a (c Paint.left)))
abbrev dftOutKind := a (c Paint.left)
abbrev convInKind := p w (p sc (p (a (c Paint.left)) (a (c Paint.right))))
abbrev convOutKind := a (c Paint.both)

def DFTProgram (order : Prog false w w) (solve : Prog false dftInKind dftOutKind)
    (W : ℕ → ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ cBound : ℕ, ∀ n : ℕ, 0<n →
    let cap := (n+2)^cBound
    let d := run order n
    W n + 10*(n+2) ≤ cap ∧ d.peak ≤ cap ∧ d.valid ∧
    0 < d.val ∧ d.val < 1024*n^3 ∧ d.val ≤ cap ∧
    ∀ x : Vec n,
      let result := run solve (n, root d.val, (⟨n,x⟩ : Tape ℂ))
      result.valid ∧ result.peak ≤ cap ∧ d.work+1+result.work ≤ W n ∧
      result.val = (⟨n, dft n x⟩ : Tape ℂ)

def ConvProgram (order : Prog false w w) (solve : Prog true convInKind convOutKind)
    (W : ℕ → ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ cBound : ℕ, ∀ n : ℕ, 0<n →
    let cap := (n+2)^cBound
    let d := run order n
    W n + 10*(n+2) ≤ cap ∧ d.peak ≤ cap ∧ d.valid ∧
    0 < d.val ∧ d.val < 1024*(2*n-1)^3 ∧ d.val ≤ cap ∧
    ∀ x y : Vec n,
      let result := run solve (n, root d.val, (⟨n,x⟩ : Tape ℂ),(⟨n,y⟩ : Tape ℂ))
      result.valid ∧ result.peak ≤ cap ∧ d.work+1+result.work ≤ W n ∧
      result.val = (⟨2*n-1, conv n x y⟩ : Tape ℂ)

def TimeBounds (W : ℕ → ℕ) : Prop :=
  let W' := fun n : ℕ => (W n : ℝ)
  W' =O[atTop] paperTime ∧ W' =O[atTop] decimalTime ∧ W' =o[atTop] nlogn

def DFTGoal : Prop := ∃ order solve W, DFTProgram order solve W ∧ TimeBounds W

def ConvGoal : Prop := ∃ order solve W, ConvProgram order solve W ∧ TimeBounds W

/-- One fixed program computes every positive-length canonical DFT, including preparation. -/
theorem transform_main : DFTGoal := by
  sorry

/-- One fixed bilinear program computes convolution with the same asymptotic bounds. -/
theorem convolution_main : ConvGoal := by
  sorry

end
end PowerSaving

end OAI
```

    },

      "docstring": null,

      "name": "Code.run",
      "statement_lean": "noncomputable def Code.run {allow r s t} : Code allow r s t → Handler r → s.T → Bill t.T\n  | .atom op, _, x => op.run x\n  | .comp f g, h, x => ((f.run h x).pass (g.run h)).pay 1 0\n  | .fork f g, h, x => (f.run h x).pass (fun y => (g.run h x).pass (fun z => Bill.one (y,z)))\n  | .ifz q f g, h, x => ((q.run h x).pass (fun y =>\n      if (y : ℕ) = 0 then f.run h x else g.run h x)).pay 1 0\n  | .loop n init body, h, x =>\n      ((n.run h x).pass (fun l => (init.run h x).pass (fun y =>\n        Bill.steps y (fun i z => body.run h (x,(i,z))) l))).pay 1 0\n  | @Code.tab _ _ _ t n body, h, x =>\n      ((n.run h x).pass (fun l =>\n        Bill.tab l t.blank (fun i => body.run h (x,i)))).pay 1 0\n  | @Code.sow _ _ _ t n count body, h, x =>\n      ((n.run h x).pass (fun l => (count.run h x).pass (fun k =>\n        Bill.sow l k t.blank (fun i => body.run h (x,i))))).pay 1 0\n  | .importClosed f, _, x => (f.run () x).pay 1 0\n  | .call, h, x => (h x).pay 1 0\n  | .descend base step, _, (k,x) =>\n      (depthRun (base.run ()) (step.run) k x).pay 1 k"

      "statement_lean": "def DFTProgram (order : Prog false w w) (solve : Prog false dftInKind dftOutKind)\n    (W : ℕ → ℕ) : Prop :=\n  ∃ cBound : ℕ, ∀ n : ℕ, 0<n →\n    let cap := (n+2)^cBound\n    let d := run order n\n    W n + 10*(n+2) ≤ cap ∧ d.peak ≤ cap ∧ d.valid ∧\n    0 < d.val ∧ d.val < 1024*n^3 ∧ d.val ≤ cap ∧\n    ∀ x : Vec n,\n      let result := run solve (n, root d.val, (⟨n,x⟩ : Tape ℂ))\n      result.valid ∧ result.peak ≤ cap ∧ d.work+1+result.work ≤ W n ∧\n      result.val = (⟨n, dft n x⟩ : Tape ℂ)"

    "text": "The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):\n\n- [Finite tensor savings and exact Fourier circuits](../../preprints/Finite-tensor-savings-and-exact-Fourier-circuits-September-25-2026/main.pdf)\n- [An explicit power saving for the exact discrete Fourier transform](../../preprints/An-explicit-power-saving-for-the-exact-discrete-Fourier-transform-September-25-2026/main.pdf)\n\n## Scope\n\nThe formalized result gives exact discrete Fourier transforms with arbitrarily small normalized circuit cost along an unbounded sequence of lengths. For every $c>0$ and every cutoff $N_0\\ge2$, some $n\\ge N_0$ has a circuit computing the unnormalized DFT with fewer than $cn\\log_2 n$ gates. Addition, subtraction, and multiplication by a predetermined complex scalar each cost one gate; diagonal scalings are charged. The result is subsequential, with no all-length, bounded-coefficient, conditioning, or bit-complexity claim.\n\nA separate uniform formalization gives fixed deterministic programs for every positive length $n$, computing the canonical discrete Fourier transform and the full convolution of two length-$n$ complex vectors exactly. Their work is $O(n(\\log n)^{1-10^{-13}})=o(n\\log n)$, including root-order selection and scalar preparation. The chosen root of unity is supplied; the model permits exact complex arithmetic and unrestricted coefficients, while integer values and indices remain polynomially bounded in $n$. The synthesis appendix's extraction, search, and printer uniformization is outside these selected statements."

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a deterministic algorithm that computes the discrete Fourier transform at every length <i>n</i> in $`O(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}})`$ operations. The model uses exact complex arithmetic, unrestricted coefficients, specified Fourier roots, and unit-cost logarithmic-size indexing; scalar preparation and array organization are included.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

For $d\ge1$, put $\zeta_d=\exp(2\pi i/d)$ and let $$F_d=(\zeta_d^{jk})_{0\le j,k<d}$$ be the unnormalized discrete Fourier matrix. Its fast computation is a basic instance of replacing a dense linear map by a short sequence of structured operations. The mixed-radix factorization of Cooley and Tukey (Cooley and Tukey 1965) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[cooleytukey](#ref-cooleytukey)] computes transforms at highly composite lengths in $O(n\log n)$ arithmetic operations. Good’s coprime-factor construction identifies certain one-dimensional transforms with tensor products, without intervening twiddle factors (Good 1958, secs. 11–12) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[good1958](#ref-good1958)]; Cooley, Lewis, and Welch explain its relationship with Thomas’s construction and the mixed-radix FFT (Cooley, Lewis, and Welch 1967) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[cooleyLewisWelch1967](#ref-cooleyLewisWelch1967)]. Bluestein’s chirp identity reduces an arbitrary Fourier length to convolution and, with an ordinary FFT, yields the $O(n\log n)$ arithmetic bound at every length (Bluestein 1970) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[bluestein](#ref-bluestein)]. Rabiner, Schafer, and Rader give the weighted-convolution derivation, padding, and operation count (Rabiner, Schafer, and Rader 1969, secs. II–III) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[rabinerSchaferRader1969](#ref-rabinerSchaferRader1969)].

Improvements within that scale and improvements of the scale itself are different questions. Alman and Rao use matrix non-rigidity to improve the Walsh–Hadamard transform and then the leading constant for power-of-two discrete Fourier transforms (Alman and Rao 2023, Theorem 1.2 and Section 7) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[almanrao](#ref-almanrao)]. Their Fourier bound has leading term $(15/4)n\log_2 n$ in a count of real arithmetic operations, with circuit structure and constants precomputed. Here we obtain a power saving in the logarithm at every length, in an exact complex-arithmetic model that also counts scalar preparation and array organization.

Lower bounds at the $n\log n$ scale depend on the permitted operations. Morgenstern’s determinant argument applies to the unnormalized Fourier transform with bounded coefficients (Morgenstern 1973) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[morgenstern](#ref-morgenstern)]. Ailon’s entropy bound concerns the normalized transform on $n$ coordinates with unitary two-coordinate gates (Ailon 2013, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ailon](#ref-ailon)]; his later bounds also quantify restrictions on intermediate conditioning (Ailon 2014) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[ailonConditioned](#ref-ailonConditioned)]. Our model permits unrestricted coefficients and exact intermediate values. The logarithmic word size below describes integer addresses; it does not limit the precision of complex registers.

The main obstacle is to turn a saving for one fixed finite matrix into a single algorithm for arbitrary Fourier lengths. A finite saving must survive three transitions: using all available coordinates during recursive batching, compiling small Fourier matrices without increasing their widths, and organizing a product of many coordinate sets with linear array work. The complexity of preparing the scalars and constructing the schedules must also be counted. The theorem below handles these transitions explicitly.

Two companion results clarify the starting point. The finite tensor saving and the nonuniform subsequence theorem in *Finite tensor savings and exact Fourier circuits* (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[exactfourier](#ref-exactfourier)] have an independent proof through matrix prices and exact finite identities. The complex phase network in *Integer multiplication below $n\log n$* (OpenAI 2026b, Proposition 8 and Section 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[motif](#ref-motif)] provides a particular two-coordinate kernel with a strict saving. We reproduce the network needed here and supply its uniform array implementation. The integer-multiplication article’s arithmetic model and its later tape and precision arguments are separate from the model used in this paper.

<a id="sec:model"></a>
#### Arithmetic model and main result

Our model has complex registers and integer address registers. A complex field operation has unit cost. In the computation on the input vector, only addition, subtraction, and multiplication by prepared, input-independent scalars are used. Every such operation is charged. Division is used in scalar preparation only, with a proof that its denominator is nonzero. Integer operations and random access on $O(\log(n+2))$-bit words also have unit cost; a fixed number of such words may represent an address or integer. All constants implicit in these conventions are independent of $n$.

We supply an explicitly specified Fourier root depending on $n$. Equivalently, a root-of-unity operation returns the specified $\zeta_d$, at unit cost, for a stated order $d$. The theorem below needs only one such root, of polynomial order. Preparing other scalars from it, constructing the schedule, and organizing the arrays are included in the running time. We do not assume that field operations on rational constants can generate roots of arbitrarily large order. There is no bound on complex coefficients or intermediate magnitudes, and no finite-precision or numerical-stability assertion.

<a id="eq:main-constants"></a>
Define the absolute constants $$\label{eq:main-constants}
 m=10^6,\qquad W_*=2^{71},\qquad
 \Delta=6871402692000000,\qquad
 \lambda=m-\frac{\Delta}{W_*},\qquad
 \theta=\log_m\lambda.$$ The construction in Section <a href="#sec:network" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:network">[sec:network]</a> uses blocks of $m$ binary coordinates and $W_*$ arrays. The saving $\Delta$ gives the recurrence multiplier $\lambda$ and hence the exponent $\theta$. These constants satisfy $1<\lambda<m$ and $$\theta=0.99999999999978935615699598\ldots<1.$$ The decimal is included only to indicate the size of the saving; the definition in <a href="#eq:main-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-constants">[eq:main-constants]</a> is exact.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main-bound"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *There is a single deterministic algorithm which, for every positive integer $n$ and every $x\in\mathbb C^n$, computes $F_nx$ exactly. In the model above, including scalar preparation, schedule construction, and index work, its running time is $$\label{eq:main-bound}
 O\!\left(n(\log n)^\theta(\log\log n)^{4-\theta}\right)$$ as $n\to\infty$. It suffices to supply the specified root $\zeta_{D_*}$ for an explicitly computable integer $D_*<1024n^3$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-decimal"></a>
<div id="cor:decimal" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *In the same model, the transform is computable at every length in $$O\!\left(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}}\right)=o(n\log n)$$ time.*

</div>

A finite initial range is handled by the direct formula for $F_n$. The constants in the algorithm are enormous, so these are asymptotic existence and explicitness results, with no useful crossover estimate. The hypotheses on coefficients and exact roots are part of the result. In particular, the conclusion does not assert a corresponding improvement for stable floating-point FFTs or for bit complexity.

<a id="stmt-cor-polynomial-multiplication"></a>
<div id="cor:polynomial-multiplication" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3** (Exact convolution and polynomial multiplication). *There is a single deterministic algorithm which, for every positive integer $n$ and every $a,b\in\mathbb C^n$, computes the linear convolution $$c_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le r,s<n\\r+s=k}}a_rb_s,
 \qquad 0\le k\le 2n-2.$$ Equivalently, it multiplies two complex polynomials of degree less than $n$. In the exact complex field-operation, specified-root, and address cost model above, with general field multiplication permitted for the pointwise products, the total running time, including scalar preparation, schedule construction, padding, and index work, is $$O\!\left(n(\log n)^\theta(\log\log n)^{4-\theta}\right)
 \qquad(n\to\infty),$$ and hence is eventually $O(n(\log n)^{1-10^{-13}})$. This is a bilinear arithmetic algorithm, not an input-linear circuit: its pointwise products multiply a value linear in $a$ by a value linear in $b$.*

</div>

The proof in Section <a href="#sec:convolution-consequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:convolution-consequence">5.5</a> uses three transforms at length $2n-1$, with one specified root reused throughout.

The corollary retains the exact-root and unrestricted-coefficient assumptions. It is not a result about integer bit complexity, bounded-coefficient circuits, numerical stability, finite fields, or formal power-series algorithms.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The construction and its reusable parts

Two constructions feed a common tensor schedule, which then supplies the transform at every length.

1.  A fixed network gives a fast algorithm for tensor powers of $$C=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}1+i&1-i\\1-i&1+i\end{pmatrix}.$$ Its binary labels prescribe changes of frame between scalar gates. The completed network transforms every physical role, including roles used as temporary storage. Padding the number of roles to a power of two makes recursive batching exact. The resulting recurrence gives $C^{\otimes k}$ in $O(2^k(k+1)^\theta)$ time.

2.  Independently, Newton interpolation factors every Fourier matrix $F_r$ into diagonal factors and a triangular Toeplitz matrix and its transpose. We compile these into a word of $O(1+\log^4r)$ layers on *exactly* $r$ coordinates. Borrowed coordinates may initially contain arbitrary data and are restored. The compiler chooses chunk sizes by counting the registers required by its own convolution programs; all local compilation and scalar preparation take polynomial time in $r$.

3.  Synchronize these words on many coprime axes. Each simultaneous pair layer decomposes into sectors carrying tensor powers of $C$. A concrete sector-address formula packs all sectors in linear array work. Products of small odd primes, supplemented by one power of two, give suitable working lengths within a factor of two of $2n$. A chirp convolution then gives every requested length.

The role-preserving tensor recurrence, the measured-fit local compiler, and the linear-time sector traversal are useful independently of the final choice of Fourier lengths. Together they prevent three possible losses: allocating fresh zero workspace at every recursive use, silently treating scalar preparation as free, and spending one operation per tensor axis at every array entry. The sharper logarithmic factor in <a href="#eq:main-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-bound">[eq:main-bound]</a> comes from the elementary estimate that the number of odd-prime factors is of order $\log n/\log\log n$. The appendix supplies two further general constructions: finite rational-algebra extraction from polynomial certificates, and bounded-printer uniformization of effectively generated transform circuits.

Figure <a href="#fig:proof-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-map">1</a> records the interfaces between these steps.

<figure id="fig:proof-map">

<figcaption>The quantitative proof. The tensor-power algorithm and the exact-width Fourier words are separate inputs to synchronization. Both constructions account for arbitrary data in their auxiliary roles. Appendix <a href="#sec:synthesis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:synthesis">[sec:synthesis]</a> gives the separate certificate-and-printer route.</figcaption>
</figure>

- Top-right node contains: "Exact-width Fourier words", "Proposition 3.1:", "$O(1 + \log^4 r)$ common slots", "on exactly $r$ coordinates".

Section <a href="#sec:network" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:network">[sec:network]</a> develops the fixed network. Section <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">[sec:local]</a> gives the local Fourier compiler, Section <a href="#sec:synchronization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:synchronization">4</a> handles tensor scheduling and addresses, and Section <a href="#sec:all-lengths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:all-lengths">5</a> proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and its convolution consequence. Appendix <a href="#sec:synthesis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:synthesis">[sec:synthesis]</a> gives a complementary effective route from finite algebraic certificates: it makes finite-win certificates uniform by exact rational algebra and supplies a local certificate that proves the search terminates. The general finite-win and generation results of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[exactfourier](#ref-exactfourier)] give a second source of the same certificate interface. That route is not needed for the exponent in <a href="#eq:main-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-bound">[eq:main-bound]</a>.

The finite network is credited at its use to (OpenAI 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[motif](#ref-motif)], and the exact-width Fourier mechanism is shared with (OpenAI 2026a, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[exactfourier](#ref-exactfourier)]. We prove the interfaces used here, including their preparation and indexing bounds. In particular, we do not import the later tape or precision bounds of (OpenAI 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[motif](#ref-motif)], nor infer uniformity from the circuit-existence conclusion of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[exactfourier](#ref-exactfourier)].

<a id="net:section"></a>
### An explicit saving for a fixed two-coordinate transform

<span id="sec:network" label="sec:network"></span>

<a id="net:C"></a>
Put $$\label{net:C}
 C=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&a\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad a=\frac{1+i}{2},\qquad b=\frac{1-i}{2}.$$ The ordinary algorithm applies the $k$ factors of $C^{\otimes k}$ separately, using $O(k2^k)$ operations. We obtain a smaller exponent of $k$ by a fixed network from the complex construction of (OpenAI 2026b, Proposition 8 and Section 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[motif](#ref-motif)]. The network exchanges two banks and restores arbitrary auxiliary values. Changing the coordinate frame along its wires then transforms *every* bank and auxiliary array. The total dimension of the frame changes is smaller than the cost of transforming the arrays separately. We give the complete finite construction because this strict saving and the treatment of arbitrary auxiliaries are the main inputs to the recursion.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### A scalar network with arbitrary auxiliary values

Fix <a id="net:parameters"></a>$$\label{net:parameters}
 h=100,\quad
 \mathcal T=\{T\subseteq\{1,\ldots,h\}:|T|=3\},\quad
 v=\binom h3=161700,\quad N_0=v^3,\quad m=h^3=10^6.$$ There are two banks of scalar wires $X_d,Y_d$, indexed by $d=(d_1,d_2,d_3)\in\mathcal T^3$. Two triples are *neighbors* when they have even intersection; neighboring triples are distinct. The network has three stages. At stage $j$, fix the two indices other than $d_j$ and operate on the two lists obtained by varying $d_j$. There are $I_0=3v^2$ such invocations. Each has its own auxiliary wires, distinct from all other invocations: one side wire $A_{ST}$ for each *ordered* neighboring pair $(S,T)$, and $h+1$ central wires $c_1,\ldots,c_h,c_*$.

<a id="net:schedule"></a>
For one invocation, call the logical source and target banks $x=(x_T)$ and $y=(y_S)$. Define the fixed linear maps $$\begin{aligned}
 (Vx)_{ST}&=x_T,&
 (Gx)_j&=\sum_{T\ni j}x_T,& (Gx)_*&=\sum_Tx_T,\\
 (JA)_S&=-\sum_{T:\,T\text{ neighbors }S}
                  \frac{|S\cap T|-1}{2}A_{ST},&
 (Rc)_S&=\frac{\sum_{j\in S}c_j-c_*}{2}.
\end{aligned}$$ The forward invocation consists of the following eight rows, in order: $$\label{net:schedule}
\begin{array}{c|l|l}
 \text{row}&\text{update}&\text{gate grouping}\\\hline
 0&y\gets y-JA&\text{one gate per target and its side wires}\\
 1&y\gets y-Rc&\text{one gate on the targets and center}\\
 2&A\gets A+Vx&\text{one gate per source and its side wires}\\
 3&c\gets c+Gx&\text{one gate on the sources and center}\\
 4&y\gets y+Rc&\text{one gate on the targets and center}\\
 5&y\gets y+JA&\text{one gate per target and its side wires}\\
 6&c\gets c-Gx&\text{one gate on the sources and center}\\
 7&A\gets A-Vx&\text{one gate per source and its side wires}.
\end{array}$$ A gate is simply the indicated finite linear map on its wires. Its scalar implementation, including all additions and multiplications, has a fixed finite cost.

<a id="stmt-net-scalar"></a>
<div id="net:scalar" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *For arbitrary initial auxiliary values, the forward invocation sends $(x,y,A,c)$ to $(x,y+x,A,c)$. Forward invocations from $X$ to $Y$ at stages $1$ and $3$, and the inverse forward invocation from $Y$ to $X$ at stage $2$, give $$(X_d,Y_d)\longmapsto(-Y_d,X_d)
 \quad(d\in\mathcal T^3)$$ and restore every auxiliary value.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-net-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-net-scalar</summary>

*Proof.* The first two rows subtract $JA+Rc$ from $y$. The middle rows add $R(c+Gx)+J(A+Vx)$, and the last two restore $c$ and $A$. The net addition is $(RG+JV)x$. The coefficient from $x_T$ to $y_S$ in $RG$ is $(|S\cap T|-1)/2$. It is $1$ for $S=T$, $0$ when the intersection has size $1$, and is canceled by $JV$ when the intersection has size $0$ or $2$. Hence $RG+JV=I$. The three stages therefore send $(x,y)$ to $(x,y+x)$, then $(-y,y+x)$, then $(-y,x)$. The auxiliary values cancel in each invocation separately. ◻

</details>

A triple has $$d_{\mathrm{nbr}}=\binom{97}{3}+3\cdot97=147731$$ neighbors: the two terms count intersections of size $0$ and $2$. Consequently the number of physical wires is <a id="net:wire-count"></a>$$\label{net:wire-count}
 W=2N_0+I_0(vd_{\mathrm{nbr}}+101)
   =1873807244643542670000.$$ The factor $vd_{\mathrm{nbr}}$ counts ordered pairs, as required by the schedule.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Frames turn binary dimensions into directional operations

We now explain how to turn the scalar exchange into a tensor transform. An edge of the scalar network connects consecutive touches of a physical wire, including its source and sink. We will assign a binary subspace to each gate and terminal, with one common subspace for all wires touched by a gate. This subspace specifies a change of frame on the complex array replacing each wire. The construction below has two useful properties: common frames commute with scalar gates, and moving between suitable nested subspaces costs one directional operation per added or removed binary dimension.

For this purpose use the binary space $\mathcal D=\mathbb F_2^m$ with its ordinary dot product. A subspace is *nondegenerate* if its restricted bilinear form has zero radical. Its orthogonal complement and all projections below are taken over $\mathbb F_2$; these are separate from the complex spaces carrying the data.

For $z\in\mathbb F_2^m$, write $R_z$ for translation of array addresses: $(R_zf)(x)=f(x+z)$. The normalized Walsh matrix $$J_m=2^{-m/2}\bigl((-1)^{x\cdot y}\bigr)_{x,y\in\mathbb F_2^m}$$ satisfies $J_m^2=I$. Conjugating the diagonal character $(-1)^{z\cdot x}$ by $J_m$ gives $R_z$, by cancellation of nontrivial binary characters. For a nondegenerate label $U$, let $P_U$ be projection onto $U$ along $U^\perp$, and set <a id="net:frame"></a>$$\label{net:frame}
 q_U(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(P_Ux)\pmod4,\qquad
 \Phi_U=J_m\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_x(i^{q_U(x)})J_m.$$ Here $\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}$ is integer Hamming weight. These matrices prove identities; the algorithm does not execute Walsh transforms. In particular $\Phi_0=I$ and $\Phi_{\mathcal D}=C^{\otimes m}$.

<a id="stmt-net-edge"></a>
<div id="net:edge" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *Let $U,V\subseteq\mathcal D$ be nested nondegenerate subspaces. Call the orthogonal complement of the smaller subspace inside the larger their *residual*. Suppose it has an orthonormal binary basis of size $\rho$. Then $\Phi_V\Phi_U^{-1}$ is a product of exactly $\rho$ *directional kernels* of the form $C_z=aI+bR_z$ or $C_z^{-1}$, where $z\ne0$. Each inverse requires one forward kernel and a translation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-net-edge"><summary>Proof of stmt-net-edge</summary>

*Proof.* For binary vectors $x,y$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(x+y)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(x)+\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(y)-2|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(x)\cap\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(y)|.$$ Thus weight is additive modulo four for orthogonal vectors. If $V=U\mathbin\perp E$ and $z_1,\ldots,z_\rho$ is an orthonormal basis of $E$, then $$q_V(x)-q_U(x)
   =\sum_{j=1}^{\rho}\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(z_j)[z_j\cdot x]\pmod4,$$ where brackets mean the integer representative $0$ or $1$. Every $\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(z_j)$ is odd, so its value modulo four is $1$ or $-1$. For a decreasing edge the entire expression is negated. The identity $i^{[z\cdot x]}=a+b(-1)^{z\cdot x}$ and Walsh conjugation give the stated kernels. Finally $R_z^2=I$ and $C_z^2=R_z$, whence $C_z^{-1}=R_zC_z$. ◻

</details>

To apply this calculation to the network, replace each scalar wire by an array indexed by $\mathcal D$. Give every gate a nondegenerate label $U$, common to all wires it touches, and give each source and sink its own label. Insert $\Phi_V\Phi_U^{-1}$ along an edge from label $U$ to label $V$, and apply the scalar gates pointwise. A scalar gate mixes its touched arrays with scalar coefficients, whereas their common frame acts on the address coordinate. These two operations commute. The intermediate frames therefore cancel, leaving the scalar network between its source and sink frames.

<a id="net:telescoping"></a>
More generally, for an integer $f\ge1$, index every array by $f$ columns of $m$ bits and use $\Phi_U^{\otimes f}$ as its frame. The same cancellation gives the complete linear map $$\label{net:telescoping}
 \mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_{\mathrm{sinks}}(\Phi_U^{\otimes f})\,
 \mathcal S\,
